I came in because I’m just… completely worn out. Not tired in the normal way, like after a bad night’s sleep or doing too much. It’s more like my body has no fuel in it at all. Even walking from the bedroom to the kitchen feels like I’ve been hauling lumber all day, like every step is heavier than it should be.

And the thing is, resting doesn’t seem to put anything back. I can sit down, lie down, try to take it easy, and I still feel drained. It’s like I’m carrying this weight around all the time, and I can’t shake it. That’s really what pushed me to come in. I just can’t keep going like this.

Yeah… I have. Pretty much every day, honestly. It’s not just feeling a little sad, like having a bad afternoon. I wake up and it’s already there before I’ve even done anything — this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest. Like the day is over before it starts.

It’s embarrassing to say that, but it just comes over me. I’ll be sitting there thinking I should get up, I should move, I should do something, and instead I’m just… stuck there crying, feeling like there’s no point to any of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it’s changed a lot. I don’t really enjoy much of anything anymore.

I used to have things I actually looked forward to, you know? Choir was one of them. I liked being around people, having something to practice, feeling like I was part of something. And pickup ball too — even if I wasn’t great, it got me moving, got me laughing a little. I used to work on cars for hours and lose track of time, and I’d grill for the family like it was a whole event, planning what I was going to make, making sure everybody had enough.

Now it’s like… I know those things are supposed to matter to me, but I can’t make myself care enough to do them. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming by, or somebody will mention playing ball, and I just let the messages sit there. I see them, and I don’t answer. Not because I’m busy. I just can’t bring myself to respond, and I don’t feel that pull to show up like I used to. It’s like the part of me that cared about those things got turned off.

Even walking from the bedroom to the kitchen feels like I’ve been hauling lumber all day, like every step is heavier than it should be.\n\nAnd the thing is, resting doesn’t seem to put anything back. I can sit down, lie down, try to take it easy, and I still feel drained. It’s like I’m carrying this weight around all the time, and I can’t shake it. That’s really what pushed me to come in. I just can’t keep going like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Pretty much every day, honestly. It’s not just feeling a little sad, like having a bad afternoon. I wake up and it’s already there before I’ve even done anything — this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest. Like the day is over before it starts.\n\nSome days I end up out in the garage just sitting there, not doing anything, and I’ll start crying. And I can’t even always tell you what set it off. It’s embarrassing to say that, but it just comes over me. I’ll be sitting there thinking I should get up, I should move, I should do something, and instead I’m just… stuck there crying, feeling like there’s no point to any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed a lot. I don’t really enjoy much of anything anymore.\n\nI used to have things I actually looked forward to, you know? Choir was one of them. I liked being around people, having something to practice, feeling like I was part of something. And pickup ball too — even if I wasn’t great, it got me moving, got me laughing a little. I used to work on cars for hours and lose track of time, and I’d grill for the family like it was a whole event, planning what I was going to make, making sure everybody had enough.\n\nNow it’s like… I know those things are supposed to matter to me, but I can’t make myself care enough to do them. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming by, or somebody will mention playing ball, and I just let the messages sit there. I see them, and I don’t answer. Not because I’m busy. I just can’t bring myself to respond, and I don’t feel that pull to show up like I used to. It’s like the part of me that cared about those things got turned off.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

I don’t know… anxious, maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m nervous about this one specific thing,” the way I might’ve before, like if I had a bill due or an appointment or something.

It’s more like everything in me is already worn down, so even small things feel like too much. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just me being… I don’t know, depleted. Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes.

Like the day is over before it starts.\n\nSome days I end up out in the garage just sitting there, not doing anything, and I’ll start crying. And I can’t even always tell you what set it off. It’s embarrassing to say that, but it just comes over me. I’ll be sitting there thinking I should get up, I should move, I should do something, and instead I’m just… stuck there crying, feeling like there’s no point to any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed a lot. I don’t really enjoy much of anything anymore.\n\nI used to have things I actually looked forward to, you know? Choir was one of them. I liked being around people, having something to practice, feeling like I was part of something. And pickup ball too — even if I wasn’t great, it got me moving, got me laughing a little. I used to work on cars for hours and lose track of time, and I’d grill for the family like it was a whole event, planning what I was going to make, making sure everybody had enough.\n\nNow it’s like… I know those things are supposed to matter to me, but I can’t make myself care enough to do them. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming by, or somebody will mention playing ball, and I just let the messages sit there. I see them, and I don’t answer. Not because I’m busy. I just can’t bring myself to respond, and I don’t feel that pull to show up like I used to. It’s like the part of me that cared about those things got turned off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… anxious, maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m nervous about this one specific thing,” the way I might’ve before, like if I had a bill due or an appointment or something.\n\nIt’s more like everything in me is already worn down, so even small things feel like too much. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just me being… I don’t know, depleted. I haven’t really been noticing nervousness as the main thing. The main thing is just this heaviness, like I can’t get myself going.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not just some passing thing like, “I wish things were easier.” It’s more like this thought keeps coming into my head that I should just be dead. Like everybody would be better off if I wasn’t here, or like there’s no real reason for me to keep going through the same day over and over feeling like this.\n\nAnd when I’m around people, I can kind of push it down a little, or at least I’m distracted enough that it’s not right in front of me. But when I’m alone, that’s when it gets scary, because it starts feeling less like just a thought and more like… I don’t know… like I might actually do something to make it happen. I don’t want to say that, because it sounds awful, and I don’t want anybody looking at me like I’m crazy or weak. But yeah, I’ve been having those thoughts, and they scare me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It’s been… about four months, I think. Around there.

It kind of started after my wife went back to working full-time. Before that, she was around more during the day, or at least there was more activity in the house, more of a routine that wasn’t just me sitting with myself. Once she went back full-time, I started spending most weekdays alone, and that’s when it really settled in. At first I thought maybe I was just adjusting, like maybe I just needed to get used to the quiet or find things to do with my time. But it didn’t lift.

So yeah… about four months. It feels longer, honestly, because the days sort of run together, but if I’m trying to mark when it really changed, that’s when.

I’m… not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way, like writing down when I sleep or what I eat, so it all kind of blurs together.

Sleep-wise, I know it doesn’t feel normal, but I don’t know if I can say exactly how it changed or when. Some nights I feel like I’m in bed forever and not really resting, and then other times I just lose sense of what was sleep and what was just lying there. But I don’t have a clear number for you, if that’s what you mean.

With appetite… I don’t know. I eat when food is there sometimes, and other times I don’t really think about it. I haven’t been paying attention to whether it’s more or less than before. My wife would probably notice better than I would, honestly. I just know everything feels off, but I can’t separate it into neat categories.

People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming by, or somebody will mention playing ball, and I just let the messages sit there. I see them, and I don’t answer. Not because I’m busy. I just can’t bring myself to respond, and I don’t feel that pull to show up like I used to. It’s like the part of me that cared about those things got turned off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… anxious, maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m nervous about this one specific thing,” the way I might’ve before, like if I had a bill due or an appointment or something.\n\nIt’s more like everything in me is already worn down, so even small things feel like too much. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just me being… I don’t know, depleted. I haven’t really been noticing nervousness as the main thing. The main thing is just this heaviness, like I can’t get myself going.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not just some passing thing like, “I wish things were easier.” It’s more like this thought keeps coming into my head that I should just be dead. Like everybody would be better off if I wasn’t here, or like there’s no real reason for me to keep going through the same day over and over feeling like this.\n\nAnd when I’m around people, I can kind of push it down a little, or at least I’m distracted enough that it’s not right in front of me. But when I’m alone, that’s when it gets scary, because it starts feeling less like just a thought and more like… I don’t know… like I might actually do something to make it happen. I don’t want to say that, because it sounds awful, and I don’t want anybody looking at me like I’m crazy or weak. But yeah, I’ve been having those thoughts, and they scare me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months, I think. Around there.\n\nIt kind of started after my wife went back to working full-time. Before that, she was around more during the day, or at least there was more activity in the house, more of a routine that wasn’t just me sitting with myself. Once she went back full-time, I started spending most weekdays alone, and that’s when it really settled in. At first I thought maybe I was just adjusting, like maybe I just needed to get used to the quiet or find things to do with my time. But it didn’t lift.\n\nSo yeah… about four months. It feels longer, honestly, because the days sort of run together, but if I’m trying to mark when it really changed, that’s when.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the past four months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way, like writing down when I sleep or what I eat, so it all kind of blurs together.\n\nSleep-wise, I know it doesn’t feel normal, but I don’t know if I can say exactly how it changed or when. Some nights I feel like I’m in bed forever and not really resting, and then other times I just lose sense of what was sleep and what was just lying there. But I don’t have a clear number for you, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWith appetite… I don’t know. I eat when food is there sometimes, and other times I don’t really think about it. I haven’t been paying attention to whether it’s more or less than before. My wife would probably notice better than I would, honestly. I just know everything feels off, but I can’t separate it into neat categories.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been pretty steady, honestly… at least the way I’d describe it. Like, I can point to when it really started getting bad, which was about four months ago, when my wife went back to working full-time and I was suddenly by myself most weekdays. That’s the marker in my head.

Since then, I don’t know that I’ve had some clear stretch where I thought, “Oh, I’m getting better now,” or anything like that. There might be moments where I’m distracted or the day isn’t quite as heavy, but the overall thing has just… stayed. The alone time during the week is when it seems to press down the most, but I wouldn’t say it fully comes and goes. It’s more like it settled in around then and hasn’t really let up.

It’s like the part of me that cared about those things got turned off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… anxious, maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m nervous about this one specific thing,” the way I might’ve before, like if I had a bill due or an appointment or something.\n\nIt’s more like everything in me is already worn down, so even small things feel like too much. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just me being… I don’t know, depleted. I haven’t really been noticing nervousness as the main thing. The main thing is just this heaviness, like I can’t get myself going.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not just some passing thing like, “I wish things were easier.” It’s more like this thought keeps coming into my head that I should just be dead. Like everybody would be better off if I wasn’t here, or like there’s no real reason for me to keep going through the same day over and over feeling like this.\n\nAnd when I’m around people, I can kind of push it down a little, or at least I’m distracted enough that it’s not right in front of me. But when I’m alone, that’s when it gets scary, because it starts feeling less like just a thought and more like… I don’t know… like I might actually do something to make it happen. I don’t want to say that, because it sounds awful, and I don’t want anybody looking at me like I’m crazy or weak. But yeah, I’ve been having those thoughts, and they scare me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months, I think. Around there.\n\nIt kind of started after my wife went back to working full-time. Before that, she was around more during the day, or at least there was more activity in the house, more of a routine that wasn’t just me sitting with myself. Once she went back full-time, I started spending most weekdays alone, and that’s when it really settled in. At first I thought maybe I was just adjusting, like maybe I just needed to get used to the quiet or find things to do with my time. But it didn’t lift.\n\nSo yeah… about four months. It feels longer, honestly, because the days sort of run together, but if I’m trying to mark when it really changed, that’s when.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the past four months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way, like writing down when I sleep or what I eat, so it all kind of blurs together.\n\nSleep-wise, I know it doesn’t feel normal, but I don’t know if I can say exactly how it changed or when. Some nights I feel like I’m in bed forever and not really resting, and then other times I just lose sense of what was sleep and what was just lying there. But I don’t have a clear number for you, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWith appetite… I don’t know. I eat when food is there sometimes, and other times I don’t really think about it. I haven’t been paying attention to whether it’s more or less than before. My wife would probably notice better than I would, honestly. I just know everything feels off, but I can’t separate it into neat categories.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your depression seemed to worsen or improve slightly over the past four months?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steady, honestly… at least the way I’d describe it. Like, I can point to when it really started getting bad, which was about four months ago, when my wife went back to working full-time and I was suddenly by myself most weekdays. That’s the marker in my head.\n\nSince then, I don’t know that I’ve had some clear stretch where I thought, “Oh, I’m getting better now,” or anything like that. There might be moments where I’m distracted or the day isn’t quite as heavy, but the overall thing has just… stayed. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that’s been hardest to explain, because it’s not just that I don’t feel like doing things. It’s like my whole body has slowed down. Everything takes so much effort, even small things.

I’ll be sitting there knowing I should get up, or answer someone, or start something simple, and it feels like I’m trying to move through mud. Like there’s this delay between deciding to do something and actually being able to make my body do it. Even walking across the room feels slower than it should.

And when people talk to me, I hear them. It’s not that I don’t hear the question. I do. But it takes me a long time to get the words out. My wife will ask me something, and I’m just sitting there, trying to answer, and it’s like the words are stuck somewhere. I know that probably looks strange from the outside, like I’m ignoring people or not paying attention, but inside I’m trying. It just doesn’t come out right away.

So yeah… I’ve noticed that. A lot.

It’s not just feeling a little sad, like having a bad afternoon. I wake up and it’s already there before I’ve even done anything — this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest. Like the day is over before it starts.\n\nSome days I end up out in the garage just sitting there, not doing anything, and I’ll start crying. And I can’t even always tell you what set it off. It’s embarrassing to say that, but it just comes over me. I’ll be sitting there thinking I should get up, I should move, I should do something, and instead I’m just… stuck there crying, feeling like there’s no point to any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed a lot. I don’t really enjoy much of anything anymore.\n\nI used to have things I actually looked forward to, you know? Choir was one of them. I liked being around people, having something to practice, feeling like I was part of something. And pickup ball too — even if I wasn’t great, it got me moving, got me laughing a little. I used to work on cars for hours and lose track of time, and I’d grill for the family like it was a whole event, planning what I was going to make, making sure everybody had enough.\n\nNow it’s like… I know those things are supposed to matter to me, but I can’t make myself care enough to do them. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming by, or somebody will mention playing ball, and I just let the messages sit there. I see them, and I don’t answer. Not because I’m busy. I just can’t bring myself to respond, and I don’t feel that pull to show up like I used to. It’s like the part of me that cared about those things got turned off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… anxious, maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m nervous about this one specific thing,” the way I might’ve before, like if I had a bill due or an appointment or something.\n\nIt’s more like everything in me is already worn down, so even small things feel like too much. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just me being… I don’t know, depleted. I haven’t really been noticing nervousness as the main thing. The main thing is just this heaviness, like I can’t get myself going.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not just some passing thing like, “I wish things were easier.” It’s more like this thought keeps coming into my head that I should just be dead. Like everybody would be better off if I wasn’t here, or like there’s no real reason for me to keep going through the same day over and over feeling like this.\n\nAnd when I’m around people, I can kind of push it down a little, or at least I’m distracted enough that it’s not right in front of me. But when I’m alone, that’s when it gets scary, because it starts feeling less like just a thought and more like… I don’t know… like I might actually do something to make it happen. I don’t want to say that, because it sounds awful, and I don’t want anybody looking at me like I’m crazy or weak. But yeah, I’ve been having those thoughts, and they scare me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months, I think. Around there.\n\nIt kind of started after my wife went back to working full-time. Before that, she was around more during the day, or at least there was more activity in the house, more of a routine that wasn’t just me sitting with myself. Once she went back full-time, I started spending most weekdays alone, and that’s when it really settled in. At first I thought maybe I was just adjusting, like maybe I just needed to get used to the quiet or find things to do with my time. But it didn’t lift.\n\nSo yeah… about four months. It feels longer, honestly, because the days sort of run together, but if I’m trying to mark when it really changed, that’s when.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the past four months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way, like writing down when I sleep or what I eat, so it all kind of blurs together.\n\nSleep-wise, I know it doesn’t feel normal, but I don’t know if I can say exactly how it changed or when. Some nights I feel like I’m in bed forever and not really resting, and then other times I just lose sense of what was sleep and what was just lying there. But I don’t have a clear number for you, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWith appetite… I don’t know. I eat when food is there sometimes, and other times I don’t really think about it. I haven’t been paying attention to whether it’s more or less than before. My wife would probably notice better than I would, honestly. I just know everything feels off, but I can’t separate it into neat categories.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your depression seemed to worsen or improve slightly over the past four months?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steady, honestly… at least the way I’d describe it. Like, I can point to when it really started getting bad, which was about four months ago, when my wife went back to working full-time and I was suddenly by myself most weekdays. That’s the marker in my head.\n\nSince then, I don’t know that I’ve had some clear stretch where I thought, “Oh, I’m getting better now,” or anything like that. There might be moments where I’m distracted or the day isn’t quite as heavy, but the overall thing has just… stayed. The alone time during the week is when it seems to press down the most, but I wouldn’t say it fully comes and goes. It’s more like it settled in around then and hasn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get started on things than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that’s been hardest to explain, because it’s not just that I don’t feel like doing things. It’s like my whole body has slowed down. Everything takes so much effort, even small things.\n\nI’ll be sitting there knowing I should get up, or answer someone, or start something simple, and it feels like I’m trying to move through mud. Like there’s this delay between deciding to do something and actually being able to make my body do it. Even walking across the room feels slower than it should.\n\nAnd when people talk to me, I hear them. It’s not that I don’t hear the question. I do. But it takes me a long time to get the words out. My wife will ask me something, and I’m just sitting there, trying to answer, and it’s like the words are stuck somewhere. I know that probably looks strange from the outside, like I’m ignoring people or not paying attention, but inside I’m trying. It just doesn’t come out right away.\n\nSo yeah… I’ve noticed that. A lot.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself, so I can’t give you a number or say, like, “I lost ten pounds” or anything like that. I don’t have that kind of clear answer.

My clothes… I don’t think they feel dramatically different, but I also haven’t been paying much attention. Some days I’ll think maybe my pants feel a little looser or maybe that’s just because I’m wearing them differently or they’ve stretched out, and then I second-guess myself. I don’t look in the mirror and track it, really.

So I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed a significant change in a way I could confidently tell you. My wife might have a better sense of it than I do.

A lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past four months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Yes… most days, yes. I know I already sort of said this, but it really is like everything has slowed down. Not just in my head, but my body too.

I’ll go to stand up or start doing something, and there’s this heaviness, like I’m moving through mud or like my limbs just don’t respond the way they used to. Even ordinary things feel drawn out. I’ll sit there for a while before I can actually get myself moving.

And talking is part of it too. If someone asks me a question, I hear them. I understand that they’re asking me something. But there’s this long pause before I can answer, like the words are stuck and I’m trying to pull them out. It probably looks like I’m not listening, but I am. It just takes me too long to get anything out. So yes… it’s been that way most days.

People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming by, or somebody will mention playing ball, and I just let the messages sit there. I see them, and I don’t answer. Not because I’m busy. I just can’t bring myself to respond, and I don’t feel that pull to show up like I used to. It’s like the part of me that cared about those things got turned off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… anxious, maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m nervous about this one specific thing,” the way I might’ve before, like if I had a bill due or an appointment or something.\n\nIt’s more like everything in me is already worn down, so even small things feel like too much. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just me being… I don’t know, depleted. I haven’t really been noticing nervousness as the main thing. The main thing is just this heaviness, like I can’t get myself going.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not just some passing thing like, “I wish things were easier.” It’s more like this thought keeps coming into my head that I should just be dead. Like everybody would be better off if I wasn’t here, or like there’s no real reason for me to keep going through the same day over and over feeling like this.\n\nAnd when I’m around people, I can kind of push it down a little, or at least I’m distracted enough that it’s not right in front of me. But when I’m alone, that’s when it gets scary, because it starts feeling less like just a thought and more like… I don’t know… like I might actually do something to make it happen. I don’t want to say that, because it sounds awful, and I don’t want anybody looking at me like I’m crazy or weak. But yeah, I’ve been having those thoughts, and they scare me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months, I think. Around there.\n\nIt kind of started after my wife went back to working full-time. Before that, she was around more during the day, or at least there was more activity in the house, more of a routine that wasn’t just me sitting with myself. Once she went back full-time, I started spending most weekdays alone, and that’s when it really settled in. At first I thought maybe I was just adjusting, like maybe I just needed to get used to the quiet or find things to do with my time. But it didn’t lift.\n\nSo yeah… about four months. It feels longer, honestly, because the days sort of run together, but if I’m trying to mark when it really changed, that’s when.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the past four months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way, like writing down when I sleep or what I eat, so it all kind of blurs together.\n\nSleep-wise, I know it doesn’t feel normal, but I don’t know if I can say exactly how it changed or when. Some nights I feel like I’m in bed forever and not really resting, and then other times I just lose sense of what was sleep and what was just lying there. But I don’t have a clear number for you, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWith appetite… I don’t know. I eat when food is there sometimes, and other times I don’t really think about it. I haven’t been paying attention to whether it’s more or less than before. My wife would probably notice better than I would, honestly. I just know everything feels off, but I can’t separate it into neat categories.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your depression seemed to worsen or improve slightly over the past four months?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steady, honestly… at least the way I’d describe it. Like, I can point to when it really started getting bad, which was about four months ago, when my wife went back to working full-time and I was suddenly by myself most weekdays. That’s the marker in my head.\n\nSince then, I don’t know that I’ve had some clear stretch where I thought, “Oh, I’m getting better now,” or anything like that. There might be moments where I’m distracted or the day isn’t quite as heavy, but the overall thing has just… stayed. The alone time during the week is when it seems to press down the most, but I wouldn’t say it fully comes and goes. It’s more like it settled in around then and hasn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get started on things than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that’s been hardest to explain, because it’s not just that I don’t feel like doing things. It’s like my whole body has slowed down. Everything takes so much effort, even small things.\n\nI’ll be sitting there knowing I should get up, or answer someone, or start something simple, and it feels like I’m trying to move through mud. Like there’s this delay between deciding to do something and actually being able to make my body do it. Even walking across the room feels slower than it should.\n\nAnd when people talk to me, I hear them. It’s not that I don’t hear the question. I do. But it takes me a long time to get the words out. My wife will ask me something, and I’m just sitting there, trying to answer, and it’s like the words are stuck somewhere. I know that probably looks strange from the outside, like I’m ignoring people or not paying attention, but inside I’m trying. It just doesn’t come out right away.\n\nSo yeah… I’ve noticed that. A lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past four months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself, so I can’t give you a number or say, like, “I lost ten pounds” or anything like that. I don’t have that kind of clear answer.\n\nMy clothes… I don’t think they feel dramatically different, but I also haven’t been paying much attention. Some days I’ll think maybe my pants feel a little looser or maybe that’s just because I’m wearing them differently or they’ve stretched out, and then I second-guess myself. I don’t look in the mirror and track it, really.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed a significant change in a way I could confidently tell you. My wife might have a better sense of it than I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving more slowly than usual most days over the past four months?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, yes. I know I already sort of said this, but it really is like everything has slowed down. Not just in my head, but my body too.\n\nI’ll go to stand up or start doing something, and there’s this heaviness, like I’m moving through mud or like my limbs just don’t respond the way they used to. Even ordinary things feel drawn out. I’ll sit there for a while before I can actually get myself moving.\n\nAnd talking is part of it too. If someone asks me a question, I hear them. I understand that they’re asking me something. But there’s this long pause before I can answer, like the words are stuck and I’m trying to pull them out. It probably looks like I’m not listening, but I am. It just takes me too long to get anything out. So yes… it’s been that way most days.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

So yes… it’s been that way most days.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling restless or having an urge to move around more than usual?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Psychomotor_Changes\nmanifestation: \"Everything I do has slowed down, like my body is moving through mud, and when people ask me something I can hear the question but it takes me a long time to get words out.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… not restless, really. It’s more the opposite. I don’t feel like I have an urge to move around or pace or anything like that.

Everything feels slowed down. Like even if I know I should get up, my body just doesn’t get there easily. It’s like I’m moving through mud, if that makes sense. I’ll sit there and think, “Okay, stand up,” or “go do that,” and there’s this long delay before I actually move.

And with talking too… if someone asks me something, I hear it, I understand the question, but it takes me a long time to answer. The words just don’t come out right away. So no, I wouldn’t describe it as restlessness. It’s more like I’m stuck or slowed down.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m just… completely worn out. Not tired in the normal way, like after a bad night’s sleep or doing too much. It’s more like my body has no fuel in it at all. Even walking from the bedroom to the kitchen feels like I’ve been hauling lumber all day, like every step is heavier than it should be.\n\nAnd the thing is, resting doesn’t seem to put anything back. I can sit down, lie down, try to take it easy, and I still feel drained. It’s like I’m carrying this weight around all the time, and I can’t shake it. That’s really what pushed me to come in. I just can’t keep going like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Pretty much every day, honestly. It’s not just feeling a little sad, like having a bad afternoon. I wake up and it’s already there before I’ve even done anything — this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest. Like the day is over before it starts.\n\nSome days I end up out in the garage just sitting there, not doing anything, and I’ll start crying. And I can’t even always tell you what set it off. It’s embarrassing to say that, but it just comes over me. I’ll be sitting there thinking I should get up, I should move, I should do something, and instead I’m just… stuck there crying, feeling like there’s no point to any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed a lot. I don’t really enjoy much of anything anymore.\n\nI used to have things I actually looked forward to, you know? Choir was one of them. I liked being around people, having something to practice, feeling like I was part of something. And pickup ball too — even if I wasn’t great, it got me moving, got me laughing a little. I used to work on cars for hours and lose track of time, and I’d grill for the family like it was a whole event, planning what I was going to make, making sure everybody had enough.\n\nNow it’s like… I know those things are supposed to matter to me, but I can’t make myself care enough to do them. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming by, or somebody will mention playing ball, and I just let the messages sit there. I see them, and I don’t answer. Not because I’m busy. I just can’t bring myself to respond, and I don’t feel that pull to show up like I used to. It’s like the part of me that cared about those things got turned off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… anxious, maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m nervous about this one specific thing,” the way I might’ve before, like if I had a bill due or an appointment or something.\n\nIt’s more like everything in me is already worn down, so even small things feel like too much. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just me being… I don’t know, depleted. I haven’t really been noticing nervousness as the main thing. The main thing is just this heaviness, like I can’t get myself going.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not just some passing thing like, “I wish things were easier.” It’s more like this thought keeps coming into my head that I should just be dead. Like everybody would be better off if I wasn’t here, or like there’s no real reason for me to keep going through the same day over and over feeling like this.\n\nAnd when I’m around people, I can kind of push it down a little, or at least I’m distracted enough that it’s not right in front of me. But when I’m alone, that’s when it gets scary, because it starts feeling less like just a thought and more like… I don’t know… like I might actually do something to make it happen. I don’t want to say that, because it sounds awful, and I don’t want anybody looking at me like I’m crazy or weak. But yeah, I’ve been having those thoughts, and they scare me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months, I think. Around there.\n\nIt kind of started after my wife went back to working full-time. Before that, she was around more during the day, or at least there was more activity in the house, more of a routine that wasn’t just me sitting with myself. Once she went back full-time, I started spending most weekdays alone, and that’s when it really settled in. At first I thought maybe I was just adjusting, like maybe I just needed to get used to the quiet or find things to do with my time. But it didn’t lift.\n\nSo yeah… about four months. It feels longer, honestly, because the days sort of run together, but if I’m trying to mark when it really changed, that’s when.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the past four months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way, like writing down when I sleep or what I eat, so it all kind of blurs together.\n\nSleep-wise, I know it doesn’t feel normal, but I don’t know if I can say exactly how it changed or when. Some nights I feel like I’m in bed forever and not really resting, and then other times I just lose sense of what was sleep and what was just lying there. But I don’t have a clear number for you, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWith appetite… I don’t know. I eat when food is there sometimes, and other times I don’t really think about it. I haven’t been paying attention to whether it’s more or less than before. My wife would probably notice better than I would, honestly. I just know everything feels off, but I can’t separate it into neat categories.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your depression seemed to worsen or improve slightly over the past four months?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steady, honestly… at least the way I’d describe it. Like, I can point to when it really started getting bad, which was about four months ago, when my wife went back to working full-time and I was suddenly by myself most weekdays. That’s the marker in my head.\n\nSince then, I don’t know that I’ve had some clear stretch where I thought, “Oh, I’m getting better now,” or anything like that. There might be moments where I’m distracted or the day isn’t quite as heavy, but the overall thing has just… stayed. The alone time during the week is when it seems to press down the most, but I wouldn’t say it fully comes and goes. It’s more like it settled in around then and hasn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get started on things than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that’s been hardest to explain, because it’s not just that I don’t feel like doing things. It’s like my whole body has slowed down. Everything takes so much effort, even small things.\n\nI’ll be sitting there knowing I should get up, or answer someone, or start something simple, and it feels like I’m trying to move through mud. Like there’s this delay between deciding to do something and actually being able to make my body do it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I can’t really say that I have, no. I mean, not in a way I know for sure. I haven’t been checking my weight, and I don’t have a number to give you.

Or at least I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say yes. My clothes don’t seem wildly different to me, though I don’t always pay close attention to that kind of thing lately.

So I guess the most honest answer is: I don’t know that I’ve lost a significant amount of weight. I wouldn’t want to say yes when I’m not sure.

I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way, like writing down when I sleep or what I eat, so it all kind of blurs together.\n\nSleep-wise, I know it doesn’t feel normal, but I don’t know if I can say exactly how it changed or when. Some nights I feel like I’m in bed forever and not really resting, and then other times I just lose sense of what was sleep and what was just lying there. I haven’t been paying attention to whether it’s more or less than before. My wife would probably notice better than I would, honestly. I don’t want to say that, because it sounds awful, and I don’t want anybody looking at me like I’m crazy or weak. But yeah, I’ve been having those thoughts, and they scare me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months, I think. Around there.\n\nIt kind of started after my wife went back to working full-time. Before that, she was around more during the day, or at least there was more activity in the house, more of a routine that wasn’t just me sitting with myself. Once she went back full-time, I started spending most weekdays alone, and that’s when it really settled in. At first I thought maybe I was just adjusting, like maybe I just needed to get used to the quiet or find things to do with my time. But it didn’t lift.\n\nSo yeah… about four months. It feels longer, honestly, because the days sort of run together, but if I’m trying to mark when it really changed, that’s when.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the past four months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way, like writing down when I sleep or what I eat, so it all kind of blurs together.\n\nSleep-wise, I know it doesn’t feel normal, but I don’t know if I can say exactly how it changed or when. Some nights I feel like I’m in bed forever and not really resting, and then other times I just lose sense of what was sleep and what was just lying there. But I don’t have a clear number for you, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWith appetite… I don’t know. I eat when food is there sometimes, and other times I don’t really think about it. I haven’t been paying attention to whether it’s more or less than before. My wife would probably notice better than I would, honestly. I just know everything feels off, but I can’t separate it into neat categories.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your depression seemed to worsen or improve slightly over the past four months?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steady, honestly… at least the way I’d describe it. Like, I can point to when it really started getting bad, which was about four months ago, when my wife went back to working full-time and I was suddenly by myself most weekdays. That’s the marker in my head.\n\nSince then, I don’t know that I’ve had some clear stretch where I thought, “Oh, I’m getting better now,” or anything like that. There might be moments where I’m distracted or the day isn’t quite as heavy, but the overall thing has just… stayed. The alone time during the week is when it seems to press down the most, but I wouldn’t say it fully comes and goes. It’s more like it settled in around then and hasn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get started on things than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that’s been hardest to explain, because it’s not just that I don’t feel like doing things. It’s like my whole body has slowed down. Everything takes so much effort, even small things.\n\nI’ll be sitting there knowing I should get up, or answer someone, or start something simple, and it feels like I’m trying to move through mud. Like there’s this delay between deciding to do something and actually being able to make my body do it. Even walking across the room feels slower than it should.\n\nAnd when people talk to me, I hear them. It’s not that I don’t hear the question. I do. But it takes me a long time to get the words out. My wife will ask me something, and I’m just sitting there, trying to answer, and it’s like the words are stuck somewhere. I know that probably looks strange from the outside, like I’m ignoring people or not paying attention, but inside I’m trying. It just doesn’t come out right away.\n\nSo yeah… I’ve noticed that. A lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past four months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself, so I can’t give you a number or say, like, “I lost ten pounds” or anything like that. I don’t have that kind of clear answer.\n\nMy clothes… I don’t think they feel dramatically different, but I also haven’t been paying much attention. Some days I’ll think maybe my pants feel a little looser or maybe that’s just because I’m wearing them differently or they’ve stretched out, and then I second-guess myself. I don’t look in the mirror and track it, really.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed a significant change in a way I could confidently tell you. I understand that they’re asking me something. But there’s this long pause before I can answer, like the words are stuck and I’m trying to pull them out. So yes… it’s been that way most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having an urge to move around more than usual?\n\nPatient: No… not restless, really. It’s more the opposite. It’s like I’m moving through mud, if that makes sense. I mean, not in a way I know for sure. I haven’t been checking my weight, and I don’t have a number to give you.\n\nIf you’re asking whether I’ve clearly noticed myself getting much thinner or people have been telling me I look like I lost a lot of weight… not really. Or at least I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say yes. My clothes don’t seem wildly different to me, though I don’t always pay close attention to that kind of thing lately.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I don’t know that I’ve lost a significant amount of weight. No… I don’t think so. My wife hasn’t said, “You’ve lost weight,” or anything that clear.

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s… it’s definitely very hard to function. I wouldn’t say I’m managing normally. I can get through small things sometimes, but the bigger picture is, no, I’m not really keeping up with my life.

The house has kind of gotten away from me. Things I used to be able to handle — basic stuff around the house, repairs, keeping things in order — I just don’t follow through anymore. I’ll notice something needs doing, and I might even think through how I’d fix it, but then it just sits there. And I had these handyman plans, things I was going to do, ways I was going to keep myself useful or maybe bring in some work, and I just… I can’t make myself do them now. It’s like the plan exists in my head, but I don’t move on it.

And my wife sees it. She’s scared to leave me alone sometimes, not because I’m doing anything dramatic right in that second, but because when the thoughts get bad, I don’t trust myself. That’s probably the part that makes it feel more than just “I’m having a hard time.” If she feels like she has to worry about me being by myself, then yeah… I’d say it’s affecting daily life pretty severely.

I wish I could say I’m still managing most things, but honestly, I’m not.

---------- turn 15 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m just… completely worn out. Not tired in the normal way, like after a bad night’s sleep or doing too much. It’s more like my body has no fuel in it at all. Even walking from the bedroom to the kitchen feels like I’ve been hauling lumber all day, like every step is heavier than it should be.\n\nAnd the thing is, resting doesn’t seem to put anything back. I can sit down, lie down, try to take it easy, and I still feel drained. It’s like I’m carrying this weight around all the time, and I can’t shake it. That’s really what pushed me to come in. I just can’t keep going like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Pretty much every day, honestly. It’s not just feeling a little sad, like having a bad afternoon. I wake up and it’s already there before I’ve even done anything — this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest. Like the day is over before it starts.\n\nSome days I end up out in the garage just sitting there, not doing anything, and I’ll start crying. And I can’t even always tell you what set it off. It’s embarrassing to say that, but it just comes over me. I’ll be sitting there thinking I should get up, I should move, I should do something, and instead I’m just… stuck there crying, feeling like there’s no point to any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed a lot. I don’t really enjoy much of anything anymore.\n\nI used to have things I actually looked forward to, you know? Choir was one of them. I liked being around people, having something to practice, feeling like I was part of something. And pickup ball too — even if I wasn’t great, it got me moving, got me laughing a little. I used to work on cars for hours and lose track of time, and I’d grill for the family like it was a whole event, planning what I was going to make, making sure everybody had enough.\n\nNow it’s like… I know those things are supposed to matter to me, but I can’t make myself care enough to do them. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming by, or somebody will mention playing ball, and I just let the messages sit there. I see them, and I don’t answer. Not because I’m busy. I just can’t bring myself to respond, and I don’t feel that pull to show up like I used to. It’s like the part of me that cared about those things got turned off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… anxious, maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m nervous about this one specific thing,” the way I might’ve before, like if I had a bill due or an appointment or something.\n\nIt’s more like everything in me is already worn down, so even small things feel like too much. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just me being… I don’t know, depleted. I haven’t really been noticing nervousness as the main thing. The main thing is just this heaviness, like I can’t get myself going.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not just some passing thing like, “I wish things were easier.” It’s more like this thought keeps coming into my head that I should just be dead. Like everybody would be better off if I wasn’t here, or like there’s no real reason for me to keep going through the same day over and over feeling like this.\n\nAnd when I’m around people, I can kind of push it down a little, or at least I’m distracted enough that it’s not right in front of me. But when I’m alone, that’s when it gets scary, because it starts feeling less like just a thought and more like… I don’t know… like I might actually do something to make it happen. I don’t want to say that, because it sounds awful, and I don’t want anybody looking at me like I’m crazy or weak. But yeah, I’ve been having those thoughts, and they scare me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months, I think. Around there.\n\nIt kind of started after my wife went back to working full-time. Before that, she was around more during the day, or at least there was more activity in the house, more of a routine that wasn’t just me sitting with myself. Once she went back full-time, I started spending most weekdays alone, and that’s when it really settled in. At first I thought maybe I was just adjusting, like maybe I just needed to get used to the quiet or find things to do with my time. But it didn’t lift.\n\nSo yeah… about four months. It feels longer, honestly, because the days sort of run together, but if I’m trying to mark when it really changed, that’s when.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the past four months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way, like writing down when I sleep or what I eat, so it all kind of blurs together.\n\nSleep-wise, I know it doesn’t feel normal, but I don’t know if I can say exactly how it changed or when. Some nights I feel like I’m in bed forever and not really resting, and then other times I just lose sense of what was sleep and what was just lying there. But I don’t have a clear number for you, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWith appetite… I don’t know. I eat when food is there sometimes, and other times I don’t really think about it. I haven’t been paying attention to whether it’s more or less than before. My wife would probably notice better than I would, honestly. I just know everything feels off, but I can’t separate it into neat categories.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your depression seemed to worsen or improve slightly over the past four months?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steady, honestly… at least the way I’d describe it. Like, I can point to when it really started getting bad, which was about four months ago, when my wife went back to working full-time and I was suddenly by myself most weekdays. That’s the marker in my head.\n\nSince then, I don’t know that I’ve had some clear stretch where I thought, “Oh, I’m getting better now,” or anything like that. There might be moments where I’m distracted or the day isn’t quite as heavy, but the overall thing has just… stayed. The alone time during the week is when it seems to press down the most, but I wouldn’t say it fully comes and goes. It’s more like it settled in around then and hasn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get started on things than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that’s been hardest to explain, because it’s not just that I don’t feel like doing things. It’s like my whole body has slowed down. Everything takes so much effort, even small things.\n\nI’ll be sitting there knowing I should get up, or answer someone, or start something simple, and it feels like I’m trying to move through mud. Like there’s this delay between deciding to do something and actually being able to make my body do it. Even walking across the room feels slower than it should.\n\nAnd when people talk to me, I hear them. It’s not that I don’t hear the question. I do. But it takes me a long time to get the words out. My wife will ask me something, and I’m just sitting there, trying to answer, and it’s like the words are stuck somewhere. I know that probably looks strange from the outside, like I’m ignoring people or not paying attention, but inside I’m trying. It just doesn’t come out right away.\n\nSo yeah… I’ve noticed that. A lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past four months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself, so I can’t give you a number or say, like, “I lost ten pounds” or anything like that. I don’t have that kind of clear answer.\n\nMy clothes… I don’t think they feel dramatically different, but I also haven’t been paying much attention. Some days I’ll think maybe my pants feel a little looser or maybe that’s just because I’m wearing them differently or they’ve stretched out, and then I second-guess myself. I don’t look in the mirror and track it, really.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed a significant change in a way I could confidently tell you. My wife might have a better sense of it than I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving more slowly than usual most days over the past four months?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, yes. I know I already sort of said this, but it really is like everything has slowed down. Not just in my head, but my body too.\n\nI’ll go to stand up or start doing something, and there’s this heaviness, like I’m moving through mud or like my limbs just don’t respond the way they used to. Even ordinary things feel drawn out. I’ll sit there for a while before I can actually get myself moving.\n\nAnd talking is part of it too. If someone asks me a question, I hear them. I understand that they’re asking me something. But there’s this long pause before I can answer, like the words are stuck and I’m trying to pull them out. It probably looks like I’m not listening, but I am. It just takes me too long to get anything out. So yes… it’s been that way most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having an urge to move around more than usual?\n\nPatient: No… not restless, really. It’s more the opposite. I don’t feel like I have an urge to move around or pace or anything like that.\n\nEverything feels slowed down. Like even if I know I should get up, my body just doesn’t get there easily. It’s like I’m moving through mud, if that makes sense. I’ll sit there and think, “Okay, stand up,” or “go do that,” and there’s this long delay before I actually move.\n\nAnd with talking too… if someone asks me something, I hear it, I understand the question, but it takes me a long time to answer. The words just don’t come out right away. So no, I wouldn’t describe it as restlessness. It’s more like I’m stuck or slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past four months?\n\nPatient: I can’t really say that I have, no. I mean, not in a way I know for sure. I haven’t been checking my weight, and I don’t have a number to give you.\n\nIf you’re asking whether I’ve clearly noticed myself getting much thinner or people have been telling me I look like I lost a lot of weight… not really. Or at least I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say yes. My clothes don’t seem wildly different to me, though I don’t always pay close attention to that kind of thing lately.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I don’t know that I’ve lost a significant amount of weight. I wouldn’t want to say yes when I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife or anyone else commented on you losing weight over the past four months?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. My wife hasn’t said, “You’ve lost weight,” or anything that clear.\n\nI mean, she may have looked at me or asked whether I’m eating enough, but I don’t remember her actually commenting that I look thinner. And nobody else has really said anything either. I haven’t been around a lot of people in a way where they’d notice, I guess, but as far as actual comments about weight loss… no, not that I can recall.\n\nI wouldn’t want to say people have noticed it if they haven’t. I just don’t have anything definite there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your depression is affecting your daily life in a way that makes it very hard to function, or are you still able to manage some of your daily activities despite feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s definitely very hard to function. I wouldn’t say I’m managing normally. I can get through small things sometimes, but the bigger picture is, no, I’m not really keeping up with my life.\n\nThe house has kind of gotten away from me. Things I used to be able to handle — basic stuff around the house, repairs, keeping things in order — I just don’t follow through anymore. I’ll notice something needs doing, and I might even think through how I’d fix it, but then it just sits there. And I had these handyman plans, things I was going to do, ways I was going to keep myself useful or maybe bring in some work, and I just… I can’t make myself do them now. It’s like the plan exists in my head, but I don’t move on it.\n\nAnd my wife sees it. She’s scared to leave me alone sometimes, not because I’m doing anything dramatic right in that second, but because when the thoughts get bad, I don’t trust myself. That’s probably the part that makes it feel more than just “I’m having a hard time.” If she feels like she has to worry about me being by myself, then yeah… I’d say it’s affecting daily life pretty severely.\n\nI wish I could say I’m still managing most things, but honestly, I’m not.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Not really, no. I mean… I can’t say I’ve noticed my clothes hanging off me or anything like that.

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know. I haven’t been tracking it, and I don’t have a number I can point to and say, “Yes, I lost this much.”

I mean, over these months I haven’t been paying close attention to my weight the way I probably should. I’m not weighing myself regularly, and like I said, I haven’t had some obvious thing where my clothes suddenly feel much bigger or someone tells me I look thinner. So if it has gone down, I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it in a clear or significant way.

So… no, not that I’ve noticed. I just don’t have anything definite about my weight decreasing.

---------- turn 17 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
